ENSC1SR. Env Sci SR Transfer Pillar

ENSC SR Pillar Course

BIOL344. The Hist and Bio of Reprd Hlth

This course is designed to teach students about the history and biology of reproductive health. Students will explore the history of reproductive health in the United States during the twentieth century. They will also explore the anatomy and function of the reproductive system(s). Topics covered in this course include: menstruation, contraception, sterilization, eugenics, pregnancy, childbirth, post-partum, breastfeeding/feeding, fertility problems and solutions, and endometriosis. Cross-listed with HIST 344. Prerequisites: Completion of the FSRC and FHCI pillar requirements. PSRC, WI. 3 credits.

ENGL325. Brit Lit I:Mdevl Eight Century

Survey of British Literature from 600 to 1785. Emphasis on anonymous early works such as Beowulf and later works by such major authors as Marie de France, Chaucer, Spencer, Sidney, Marlowe, Shakespeare, Donne, Cavendish, Milton, Dryden, Behn, Swift, Finch, Pope, Montagu, Defoe, Johnson, and Burney. Prerequisite: ENGL 205. 3 credits.

ENGL327. Brit Lit II:Rmntc Contemporary

A survey of British Literature from 1789 to the present. Special emphasis on such major authors as Blake, Wordsworth, Mary Shelley, Byron, Dickens, Tennyson, Browning, George Eliot, Wilde, Shaw, Hardy, Conrad, Yeats, Woolf, Joyce, Lawrence, T. S. Eliot, Beckett, Stevie Smith, Heaney, Barnes, and Rushdie. Prerequisite: ENGL 205. 3 credits.

HIST344. Hist and Bio of Reproduct Hlth

This course is designed to teach students about the history and biology of reproductive health. Students will explore the history of reproductive health in the United States during the twentieth century. They will also explore the anatomy and function of the reproductive system(s). Topics covered in this course include: menstruation, contraception, sterilization, eugenics, pregnancy, childbirth, post-partum, breastfeeding/feeding, fertility problems and solutions, and endometriosis. Cross-listed with BIOL 344. Prerequisite: Completion of the FSRC and FHCI Pillars. PHCI, WI. 3 credits.

COMM460. Quantitative Comm Research

Students will perform quantitative research while learning communication research processes. Students will also be exposed to introductory qualitative methods and learn to become competent critics of scholarship.  Prerequisites: COMM 101 and COMM 200 (with grades of C- or better) and senior status. 3 credits.

GEOG353. Geography of Virginia

Geographical appraisal of Virginia, including the geology, landforms, soils, climate, economic minerals, original vegetation, and the human geography of Virginia, emphasizing settlement and population, agriculture, industries and transportation. 3 credits.

BIOL343. Historical Geology

Historical geology studies the evolution of the earth and life. In this course, we will unveil the story that earth has to tell of its history by learning how to interpret the rock and fossil record. Major emphasis will be placed on principles of stratigraphy, biostratigraphy, geologic history of North America, evolution, and extinction. Prerequisites: BIOL 251 and either BIOL 288 or EASC 211. 4 credits.

BIOL365. Disease Outbreaks through Hist

Outbreaks are when an infection spreads to a particular region or incidence. Through the study of outbreaks, students will learn about fields of epidemiology, disease ecology, and pathogen biology (bacteria, viruses, and parasites). This course will explore pathogens throughout history, from the bubonic plague to the recent Covid pandemic, looking at their impact on politics, religion, medicine, art/social, and/or the environment. Prerequisite: Completion of the FSRC Pillar. PSRC, SI. 3 credits.

BIOL376. Medical Assisting

A comprehensive overview of the operational and organizational aspects of a medical practice, covering key topics such as diagnostic testing, record management, patient communication, and HIPAA regulations. Students taking the course will receive a thorough review of medical terminology and an exploration of legal and ethical problems frequently encountered in a clinical setting, equipping them to work as a medical scribe. Students will concurrently enroll in an intentionally paired internship experience (BIOL 492 or BIOL 494) involving healthcare shadowing and training. Interested students should contact the Pre-health Director or Department Chair. Corequisites: BIOL 492 or BIOL 494. 1 credit.

BIOL430. Bioassessment

This course provides the skills necessary to understand, implement, and analyze methods related to the field of bioassessment. Bioassessment is a research and enforcement tool for using macroinvertebrates, as well as physical habitat, for assessing water quality and sources of pollution in lotic (streams and rivers) systems. Bioassessment's use as a tool has its basis in federal and state legal theory in order to protect our watersheds against pollution. We will explore the ecological and legal basis for bioassessment, gain the necessary field skills, and analyze our data using modern analytical computational tools. Prerequisites: MATH 171, BIOL 251, and one of the following: ENSC 201, BIOL 330, or BIOL 341. 3 credits.

CMSC111. Comp Sci Scholars Seminar I

This course is designed to prepare students in the CS Scholars program for active scholarship by developing the skills needed for college and research success. Students will participate in community-building activities, discuss study and research skills, and practice reading academic papers. Prerequisite: Open to CS Scholars only. 1 credit.

CMSC112. Comp Sci Scholars Seminar II

This course is designed to continue preparation for active scholarship by developing the skills needed for academic success in computer science. Students will participate in community-building activities, practice reading academic papers, and practice reviewing and presenting what they have read. Prerequisites: Open to CS Scholars only. 1 credit.

CMSC317. Applied Machine Learning

This course will emphasize the practical application of machine learning algorithms across diverse disciplines. Students will explore different machine learning models and learn how to apply them to solve real-word problems using actual datasets. The course will focus on correctly applying and fine-tuning machine learning techniques. Students will build machine learning projects and interpret their results. Prerequisites: CMSC 140 OR Completion of both CMSC 160 and FQRC. PQRC, SI.

COMM462. Qualitative Comm Research

Students will perform qualitative research while learning communication research processes. Students will also be exposed to introductory quantitative methods and learn to become competent critics of scholarship. Prerequisites: COMM 101 and COMM 200 (with grades of C- or better) and Senior Status. 3 credits.

EASC343. Historical Geology

Historical geology studies the evolution of the earth and life. In this course, we will unveil the story that earth has to tell of its history by learning how to interpret the rock and fossil record. Major emphasis will be placed on principles of stratigraphy, biostratigraphy, geologic history of North America, evolution, and extinction. Prerequisites: BIOL 251 and either BIOL 288 or EASC 211. 4 credits.

EDUC421. Human Growth & Development

ELEM1XX. Elem Education Elective

ENGR252. Statics

The study of scalar and vector methods employed in the analysis of discrete and distributed force systems and their application to bodies in external equilibrium. Prerequisites: PHYS 120 or PHYS 220 with a C- or better. Prerequisite/Corequisite: MATH 261. 3 credits.

ENGR307. Intro to Sci and Eng Materials

This course will introduce the basic principles underlying the behavior and properties of materials. This course will provide the scientific foundations for an understanding of the relationships among material properties, structure and performance for the classes of engineering solids (metals, ceramics, polymers, semiconductors and composites). Concepts will be developed and applied which allow for correlation between performance and aspects of structure, from atomic through the macroscopic level. Ideas relating to atomic and larger size defects and their influence on material behavior are included. Prerequisites: PHYS 221 and MATH 261 both with a C- or better. 3 credits.

ENGR324. Thermodynamics

A study of thermal properties of matter; phenomena involved in flow of heat and performance of work. Kinetic theory and statistical mechanics are included. Prerequisite: PHYS 121 or PHYS 221 both with a grade of C- or better. Prerequisite/Corequisite: MATH 262. 3 credits.

ENGR331. AC/DC Circuits

This course is an introduction to electrical circuit theory and its application to practical direct and alternating current circuits. Topics include: Kirchhoff's laws, fundamental principles of network theorems, transient and steady-state response of RC, RL, and RCL circuits by classical methods, time-domain and frequency-domain relationships, phasor analysis and power. Prerequisites: PHYS 121 or PHYS 221 both with a grade of C- or better. Prerequisite/Corequisite: MATH 261. 4 credits.

ENGR341. Electronics

This course covers the analysis, modeling and design of electrical circuits which contain electronic devices. Topics include: behavior of devices such as p-n junction transistors and bipolar junction transistors, operational amplifiers, digital systems and data acquisition and conversion. Electronics design via a systems approach is emphasized. Students will learn to design analog circuits to specifications through laboratory problems, a design project and circuit simulation. Prerequisites: PHYS 121 or PHYS 221 both with a grade of C- or better. Prerequisites/Corequisites: MATH 261. 4 credits.

ENSC323. Environmental Justice

This course explores the history, applications, and future of environmental justice as a global concern with political and ecological implications. It addresses environmental justice as a social movement rooted in US civil rights struggles and as a framework for understanding justice in environmental contexts worldwide. Using case studies, students examine the costs of toxic disasters and resource crises. Students also examine emerging justice claims as responses to unevenly distributed environmental burdens and benefits. The course also connects concepts like sustainability and biodiversity to achieving environmental justice for both humans and nonhumans. Prerequisite: Completion of FHBS Pillar. PHBS, SI. 3 credits.

ENSC430. Bioassessment

This course provides the skills necessary to understand, implement, and analyze methods related to the field of bioassessment. Bioassessment is a research and enforcement tool for using macroinvertebrates, and physical habitat for assessing water quality and sources of pollution in lotic (streams and rivers) systems. Bioassessment’s use as a tool has its basis in federal and state legal theory in order to protect our watersheds against pollution. We will explore the ecological and legal basis for bioassessment, gain the necessary field skills, and analyze our data using modern analytical computational tools. Prerequisites: MATH 171, BIOL 251, and one of the following: ENSC 201, BIOL 330, or BIOL 341. 3 credits.

HONS251. Well-Being

The purpose of this course is to teach undergraduate students skills for having resilience in the face of commonly experienced stressors and difficulties. This course will provide you with a personalized set of strategies and skills for self-care and optimize your academic and social experiences while at Longwood University and beyond. This course will use lectures, readings, videos, discussion forums, practice exercises, and coaching to assist and encourage you in meeting the course objectives while developing your more resilient and skillful self. 3 credits.

HPEP389. Methods of Teaching Health and

This course is designed to acquaint elementary education, elementary/middle school education, and liberal studies majors with content information, organizational skills, and the methods of instruction needed to teach health education and physical education in the K-6 school. Prerequisite: EDUC 260, or EDUC 261 or SPED 202 and sophomore standing required. 3 credits.

ISCS405. OSINT Strategies

This course will evaluate cyber security open source intelligence tools that are used by organizations when performing initial stage reconnaissance, black and white hat hackers, and agencies creating DOX reports on corporations and individuals. Various tools will be leveraged to create a target profile, such as reverse imaging, social networks, IP address and geographic location identifications, etc. Supporting case studies will be examined. Prerequisite: Junior standing or higher. 3 credits.

ISCS410. Cloud Infrastructure

This class provides a general overview of cloud computing and cloud security as well as hands-on work in the AWS cloud computing environment. Students will gain managerial insights in cloud computing and also complete hands-on labs that manipulate and configure primary cloud systems in AWS. Emphasis is on applying and securing information systems in the cloud. Prerequisites: ISCS 371 or CMSC 360; or permission of instructor. 3 credits.  

MAED1XX. Math Education Elective

MATH101. Introduction to Mathematics

This course introduces students to the mathematics department. Students will encounter various technologies, begin to solve problems in a mathematical way, and develop a plan for success. 1 credit.

MATH200. Test Prep through Problems

This class will focus on problem solving topics that relate to standardized tests, using the Praxis as the prototype.  Strategies for test taking success will be discussed throughout. 1 credit.

MATH250. Linear Algebra

A basic study of vector spaces, linear transformations, and their relationships to matrix algebra. Also included are determinants, eigenvalues, and eigenvectors. Prerequisites: MATH 175 or MATH 261. 3 credits.

PEDU411. Foundations of Education

This course provides an overview of the history of education's philosophical and sociological development as an institutional system in our nation and focuses on the integral role that the appreciation of cultural diversity, integration of technology, and awareness of contemporary educational trends play in teaching and learning. 3 credits.

PEDU421. Human Growth & Development

This course is an overview of the stages of human physical, social, emotional, and intellectual development and their role in designing meaningful learning experiences, social interactions, and developmentally appropriate curriculum. 3 credits.

PEDU444. Assessment of and for Learning

This course is specifically designed for PreK-12 candidates seeking an initial teaching endorsement. This course provides candidates with the knowledge base, skill sets, and tools to develop a high- quality assessment plan to meet the diverse needs of students. Virginia’s accountability system is emphasized in this course. 3 credits.

PHIL280. Philosophy of Law

This course will examine some of the basic philosophical questions surrounding the law and our legal institutions. For example: What does it take for a rule to qualify as a law? Are all laws human-made, or are some natural? Do we have a moral duty to obey the laws? When, if ever, is it justifiable to break them? When a law allows for multiple interpretations, how should judges choose among them? For what reason, if any, should those who break the law be punished? 3 credits.

PHLT301. Foundations of Traffic Safety

Foundation of Traffic Safety is the first of two courses required by the Virginia Department of Education for an endorsement in driver education. The intent of this course is to develop a thorough understanding of the highway transportation system, the complexity of the driving task, factors contributing to the performance of highway users, and attitudes and skills necessary to develop competent drivers. It will also provide prospective teachers with the essential knowledge and skills to effectively deliver the course content as presented in the Administrative and Curriculum Guide for Driver Education for Virginia. 3 credits.

PHLT302. Instr Prin Meth Clsrm In Car

Principles and Methodologies of Classroom and In-Car Instruction is the second of two courses required by the Commonwealth of Virginia for endorsement in driver education. This course provides students with an overview of teaching methods and effective practices for driver education instruction. Emphasis is placed on program organization and administration, classroom instruction, single car instruction, multiple-car range instruction, simulation and evaluation. Focus is also placed on teaching skills. The course includes a minimum of 14 hours of behind-the-wheel supervised teaching experiences. 3 credit hours. Prerequisite: HLTH 301 and a valid Virginia driver's license and driving experience.

PISC395. Special Topics

PSYC217. Psychology of Social Media

An exploration of the ways people use social media and other digital technology, and the manner in which digital media and i-technology use relates to individual development, social behavior, and sociocultural change. In the laboratory component, students will design, conduct, and interpret an empirical study of digital media use and human characteristics. Prerequisite/corequisite: PSYC 101 with a grade of C- or better. 3 credits.

SPED222. Science and Social Studies Met

This course is designed to support the development of reflective, professional, collaborative, and evidence-based practitioners.  It introduces students to the fundamentals of theory and practice for teaching science and social studies core content in K-12 education with an emphasis on unpacking standards to provide scaffolding from the foundational through mastery levels.  Curricular mapping includes evidence-based practices in delivery, lesson construction, assessment, and remediation with emphasis on developing strategies for teaching exceptional learners. 3 credits.

SPED225. Evidence-Based Pedagogy

This course is designed to introduce students to the principles and practices of evidence-based instruction within the field of special education. Students will explore various instructional strategies and learn how to apply these methods in diverse educational settings in ways that promote student engagement and success. Emphasis will be placed on critically evaluating and implementing research-supported techniques to enhance teaching and learning outcomes for students with diverse needs. Through a combination of theoretical insights and practical applications, students will gain the skills necessary to design and deliver high-quality instruction that is both evidence-based and pedagogically sound. 3 credits.

SPED251. Foundations of Autism

This course provides fundamental knowledge about individuals with autism spectrum disorder. The course emphasizes the characteristics, definitions, legislation, and history of autism.  Theories of autism, screening techniques, and intervention methods will also be explored in this course. 3 credits.

SPED275. IEP Development and Implementa

This course covers legislation and policies related to the special education evaluation process, as well as the development and implementation of Individualized Education Programs (IEPs). Various aspects of IDEA will be explored, including free and appropriate public education, non-discriminatory evaluation, and least restrictive environment. The emphasis is on the components of a well-written IEP, as well as assessing student progress. 3 credits.

SPED280. Language Development/Disorders

This course is designed to provide students with an introduction to the normal acquisition of language, and deviations in language development due to environmental and organic etiologies. Intervention approaches for language disorders for students with disabilities includes understanding low-, mid-, and high-tech assistive technology (AT) and augmentative/alternative communication (AAC) strategies.  Students will learn to assess for, design, and apply AT and AAC to facilitate success in the lives of persons with disabilities. 3 credits.

SPED316. Medical and Neurological

A comprehensive study of medical and neurological considerations, including hereditary, nutritional, and environmental factors affecting children with exceptionalities from the prenatal period through the school years. The concepts underlying various developmental disabilities concerning their characteristics due to medical and neurological factors faced by the families of children with disabilities and the intervention strategies adopted in assisting such children are also addressed. 3 credits.

SPED330. Adaptations for Exceptional

This course focuses on the development and implementation of effective, research-based adaptations and modifications for mathematics curricula tailored to meet the needs of exceptional learners. This course will equip students with the skills to adapt general educational materials and mathematics content to support diverse learning needs and ensure accessibility and engagement for all learners. Key topics include the use of differentiated instruction strategies, the integration of assistive technologies, the adaptation of assessment tools, and the development and monitoring of IEP goals. 3 credits.

SPED331. Foundations of Literacy for Ex

Research-based strategies for teaching reading and language arts to special education students in the general curriculum. This course presents an introduction to literacy terminology and concepts, as well as evidence-based practices for students with exceptional needs in a special/general education classroom. The emphasis in this course is on foundations of literacy, in particular phonological and phonemic awareness, as well as phonics instruction. 3 credits.

SPED332. Literacy Methods for Exception

Research-based strategies for teaching reading and language arts to special education students in the general curriculum, with a focus on vocabulary and comprehension, as well as teaching literacy across content areas. Prerequisite: SPED 331. Prerequsite/corequisite: SPED 333. 3 credits.

SPED333. Literacy Practicum with Except

This clinical experience course is designed for teacher candidates to engage in authentic practice opportunities with exceptional learners, specifically in the area of reading and language arts instruction. They will have the opportunity to observe, assess, plan, and teach over the course of the semester. Prerequisite: SPED 331. Prerequisite/corequisite: SPED 332. 3 credits.

SPED401. Special Education Clinical Exp

This 90-hour clinical experience course is designed for Teacher Candidates to spend time in a special education classroom, observing and participating in classroom instruction across a variety of content areas. Teacher candidates will have the opportunity to teach lessons and receive feedback. Prerequisite: Admission to Teacher Preparation. Prerequisite/Corequisite: SPED 405. 3 credits.

SPED405. Behavior Management 2

This advanced course builds upon foundational knowledge and focuses on equipping students with comprehensive techniques for effectively managing behavior in educational settings. Students will gain in-depth insights into various behavior management strategies and interventions that can be utilized to support positive behavior and address challenging behaviors in the classroom. The course covers the theoretical underpinnings of behavior management, including reinforcement, punishment, and behavior shaping, and provides practical applications for implementing these strategies effectively. Emphasis will be placed on the principles and practices of behavior modification and applied behavior analysis (ABA) specifically tailored for educators. Prerequisite: SPED 305. Prerequisite/Corequisite: SPED 401. 3 credits.

SPED406. Special Education Teaching Int

Required of all students seeking licensure to teach Special Education (grades K-12). See prerequisites for student teaching. Prerequisite: Admission to Teacher Candidacy. Corequisite: SPED 407. 10 credits.

SPED407. Special Education Teaching Int

The student teaching special education seminar class is required of all students during their student teaching semester for the purpose of collaboration with department professors during their final clinical experience in public schools before graduation.  Emphasis in the seminar course will be on unpacking the student teacher evaluations (formative, summative), processing feedback on instructional delivery and lesson planning, developing a climate of belonging in the classroom, and transference of professional dispositions to clinical practice. Prerequisite: Admission to Teacher Candidacy. Corequisite: SPED 406. 2 credits.

SPED445. Formal Assessment of Exception

This course is designed to teach students about the assessment process in special education by addressing procedural safeguards, data collection, formal procedures, issues in assessment, psychometric properties of standardized tests, and administration, scoring, and interpretation of selected instruments. The role of assessment in eligibility and the IEP process will also be discussed. Prerequisite: SPED 325. 3 credits.

SPED480. Collaboration and Transition P

This course is designed to explore techniques for including exceptional learners in the general   education classroom.  Areas of emphasis include collaborative consultation with appropriate persons in the school, home, and community and instruction in writing the transition IEP.  An in-depth study of preparatory skills for vocational opportunities needed for the workforce is also an instructional focus along with an overview of social, leisure, and recreational opportunities for exceptional learners needed for inclusive settings and transition to independent living. Prerequisite: Junior level or higher. 3 credits.

WGST391. Zines and Marginalized Comm

In this course, students examine the interdisciplinary influence of zines on culture, politics, health, and communication. Specifically, this course looks at historical and modern zine culture to gather perspective on the use of zines for communication in marginalized communities (e.g., Feminist, Civil Rights, Black, Gay Liberation, and Reproductive Rights movements). Students can expect to also create their own zines in this course. 3 credits.

WGST498. Senior Thesis I

Students conduct an in-depth project in women, gender, and sexuality studies under the direction of a faculty member and the Longwood Senior Thesis Committee. May be repeated as 499. 3 credits.

WGST499. Senior Thesis II

Students conduct an in-depth project in women, gender, and sexuality studies under the direction of a faculty member and the Longwood Senior Thesis Committee. May be repeated as 499. 3 credits.

COMM310. Digital Citizenship

This digital communication reinforcement course examines media and news literacy and criticism through multimodal analysis and creation. Throughout the course, students synthesize and engage critical stakeholders, artifacts, and platforms and how they inform decision-making, especially as it relates to media and news literacy. Students then analyze and create digital messages that critically reflect and analyze communication issues and artifacts. Prerequisite(s): COMM 141 and COMM 210. 3 credits.

FREN330. Civilization and Culture

A study of French civilization and culture with emphasis on social institutions of the current century. Prerequisite(s): FREN 210 or FREN 211 or FREN 212. 3 credits.

FREN341. Lit in French through 1789

The Middle Ages Through the Enlightenment. A study of representative works and literary movements in French literature from the Middle Ages through the Enlightenment. Students will learn to use the methods and language of literary analysis. Prerequisite(s): FREN 210 or FREN 211 or FREN 212. 3 credits.

FREN342. Lit in French since 1789

Romanticism Through the New Novel. A study of representative works and literary movements in French literature of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. Students will learn to use the methods and language of literary analysis. Prerequisite(s): FREN 210 or FREN 211 or FREN 212. 3 credits.

HONS392. Internship in Honors

ISCS370. Prncpls of Mngmnt Info Sys

An overview of information systems in the modern business environment including an exploration of Artificial Intelligence applications and how they can be used to empower organizations through increased efficiency and enabling new processes. The importance of an organization's data assets and the various ways organizations leverage analytics to understand consumer behavior, business trends, and more is stressed throughout the course. The course explores changing job roles enabled by advances in technology to prepare students in all business disciplines for post-graduate success. 3 credits.

MANG391. Professional Skills Dvlpmnt

Designed to prepare students for their required internships and ultimately a post-graduate job. Prerequisite: MANG 291 or with permission of department chair. 2 credits.

NURS100. Intro Nursing and Health Care

This foundational nursing course provides students with a comprehensive introduction to key concepts that shape the nursing profession. Through a combination of theoretical frameworks, historical context, and professional practices, students will explore the integral role of nurses within healthcare systems, both globally and domestically. The course focuses on the development of professional identity, emphasizing strategies for promoting personal health and well-being, effective communication, and ethical behavior in nursing practice. Additionally, students will examine nursing theories, regulatory processes, accreditation standards, and explore the financial and policy-related implications of healthcare systems. Prerequisite: Nursing Major or Department Chair permission. 3 credits.

NURS210. Foundations of Health Assess

This course provides theoretical knowledge and foundational skills for conducting holistic health assessments of adult and geriatric patients. Emphasis is placed on the nursing process and Clinical Judgment Model in guiding nursing practice. Students will learn to obtain a comprehensive health history, perform focused and comprehensive physical assessments, and communicate effectively using therapeutic and culturally appropriate approaches. Accurate documentation using appropriate clinical terminology is integrated throughout. Prerequisites: BIOL 206, BIOL 207, and NURS 100. 3 credits. Fee required.

NURS341. Nursing Care of Adults and Fam

This course focuses on the knowledge base necessary to provide safe, high-quality care to adults experiencing common acute and chronic health problems. The course blends knowledge from pathophysiology, pharmacology and disease management into a comprehensive plan for culturally sensitive care that integrates current evidence for best practices management. The impact of major diseases on patients and families is explored with an emphasis on the nurse's role in health promotion, symptom management and beginning interprofessional collaboration for effective care management. Simulated and actual clinical experiences take place in a variety of settings. Prerequisites: NURS 230 and NURS 240. Prerequisite/Corequisite: NURS 320. 4 credit hours lecture and 1 credit clinical. 5 credits.

POSC355. Modern Chinese Politics

This course provides students with an overview of the dynamics of the politics and policy in contemporary China. It includes topics in regard to Chinese political history, political culture, institutions, ideology, political economy, and political processes; examines issues of political change and development in China; and focuses on political fundamentals, such as the Chinese Communist Party, interest groups, protests, legal reform, as well as representation. 3 credits.

RECR220. Happiness, Well-Being, Leisure

This course examines how cultural norms, personal beliefs, and social institutions shape happiness, well-being, and leisure across the lifespan. Students will explore how assumptions and lived experiences influence leisure choices, and how those choices can either limit or promote happiness and personal growth. Through leisure education principles, self-reflection, and application, students will learn strategies for designing intentional leisure experiences that foster resilience, healthy relationships, and lifelong well-being. Prerequisites: None. 3 credits. FHBS. WI.

RECR301. TR in Mental Health

An exploration of socio-cultural and psycho-dynamic dimensions of mental health and mental illness. Students will draw from foundations of psychopathology; focus on functioning characteristics of different mental illnesses as determined by the DSM-V, and the preventative and restorative functions of therapeutic recreation and application of the therapeutic recreation process. Pre-requisite: RECR 205 and RECR 261. 3 credits.

RECR303. Rec Thrpy in Phy Reh Set

This class provides an overview of various physical disabilities and disorders that are common to clients evaluated and treated by therapeutic recreation professionals in the rehabilitation setting. Information will include disease description, etiology, pathology, clinical signs and symptoms, functional abilities and limitations. From a recreation therapy perspective, specific standardized assessments, evaluation and treatment strategies, techniques, and approaches will be addressed. Prerequisite: RECR 205. 3 credits.

RECR410. Super & Adm Leisure Services

Application of management theory and techniques of leisure service delivery, including such areas as organization, supervision techniques, financing and budget, personnel, public relations, legal foundations and liability. Prerequisites: RECR 301. 3 credits

RECR461. Issues,Trends & Research Rec

This course is the capstone course for the therapeutic recreation degree. The student will be provided opportunities for critical thinking in the process of exploring and examining current trends and issues which include evidence based research in the recreation literature. Students will engage in philosophical and knowledge base discussions through the facilitation of questions and the exchange of ideas. Prerequisites: RECR 301. 3 credits.

WGST115. Gender, Representatio, Culture

This interdisciplinary introductory course examines how gender identity, gender expression, and sexuality are constructed, understood, and challenged through cultural representations, preparing students to become culturally engaged citizens and leaders. Emphasizing media literacy and cultural criticism, students explore how creators use formal and aesthetic strategies to depict gender and sexuality across various mediums and investigate how gender and sexuality function as both personal identity and cultural markers. The course emphasizes critical evaluation and creation of representations in literature, film, digital media, and/or other cultural forms and engages contemporary debates about representation, identity, and power. Prerequisites: None. 3 credits.

BIOL345. Current Iss in Women's Biology

This course explores both reproductive and non-reproductive current issues in women’s health. In addition to reproductive issues such as contraception, reproductive technology, abortion, and parental support, we will discuss non-reproductive topics such as women’s sexuality, cancer, social determinants of health, menopause, aging, substance use, and women’s mental health. Prerequisite(s): Completion of FSRC pillar. PSRC, SI. 3 credits.

NURS325. Obstetric-Pediatric Nursing

This course introduces the principles and practices of nursing care for childbearing families and children. Students will explore the physiological, psychological, and developmental needs of women during pregnancy, childbirth, and postpartum, as well as the health needs of infants, children, and adolescents. Emphasis is placed on family-centered care, health promotion, disease prevention, and culturally sensitive practice. Students will learn to apply evidence-based interventions in both acute and community settings, integrating critical thinking and clinical judgment to support safe, holistic care. Clinical experiences will provide opportunities to develop skills in assessment, communication, and collaborative care within maternity and pediatric environments. Prerequisites: NURS 315, NURS 320, and NURS 341. Five lecture and one clinical. 6 credits.

NURS360. Evidence-Based Nursing

This course provides an overview of nursing and health care related research and evidence- based practice. It introduces the basic skills and knowledge necessary to critically read research studies and conduct a literature review using scholarly databases. Basic principles of study design are presented and ethical issues in the conduct of research  are emphasized. Strategies to promote the conduct and dissemination of nursing knowledge are explored. Prerequisite(s): NURS 230 and NURS 240; MATH 155 or MATH 171. 3 credits.

NURS372. Middle and Older Adult Care

This course integrates nursing concepts with social gerontology to explore aging complexities. Students identify common age-related changes, conditions, safety risks, and communication challenges in middle and older adults while developing strategies to support optimal functioning and preferred lifestyles. Emphasizing evidence- and theory-based management, students conduct comprehensive health assessments, identify key health and medication safety concerns, and guide tailored care plans. The course also explores health disparities, inequality, and elder abuse, neglect, or abandonment, considering social, cultural, economic, and political forces shaping aging locally and globally. Pre-requisites: NURS 320 and PSYC 230. 3 credits.

NURS393. Nursing Care of Adults II

This course focuses on the nursing care of adults experiencing complex acute and chronic health conditions. Students integrate knowledge from pathophysiology, pharmacology, and disease management to deliver evidence-based, culturally responsive, and person-centered care. Emphasis is placed on the roles of the nurse as a direct and indirect care provider, with expanded focus on care coordination, interprofessional collaboration, quality improvement, and patient safety. Students apply the nursing process and clinical judgment to promote optimal health outcomes in simulated and diverse acute care clinical settings. Three (3) lecture; one lab (1); and one (1) clinical. Prerequisites: NURS 315, NURS 320, and NURS 341. 5 credits.

NURS410. Psychiatric/Mental Health Care

This course explores nursing care for individuals with psychiatric mental health conditions across diverse treatment settings. Emphasis is placed on the theoretical frameworks and multifactorial etiologies of mental illness, along with the historical, social, cultural, spiritual, economic, and environmental factors that shape contemporary psychiatric practice. Through therapeutic communication, ethical and legal principles, and clinical judgment skills, students will learn to provide safe, evidence-based, patient-centered mental health care in partnership with individuals, families, and the interdisciplinary team. The course also evaluates the indications, benefits, limitations, and safety considerations of pharmacologic, psychotherapeutic, and integrative interventions used in modern psychiatric nursing. Prerequisite: NURS 393. 4 credits.

ACCT446. Accounting Information Systems

This course introduces students to accounting information systems (AIS) and their role in supporting financial and managerial decision-making. The course covers AIS integration with accounting principles and IT, emphasizing system documentation, database management, data analytics, and internal controls. Students explore fraud prevention, risk management, and emerging technologies like blockchain, AI, and cloud computing. Through hands-on projects and case studies, they develop technical, analytical, and communication skills essential for designing, evaluating, and using AIS effectively in modern organizations. Prerequisite: ACCT 340 or chair approval. 3 credits.

BIOL260. Introductory Biochemistry

Students in this course will explore the fundamentals of biological chemistry, including the study of macromolecules, metabolic pathways, and biochemical genetics. Prerequisite(s): CHEM 112 and either BIOL 250 or BIOL 206 and BIOL 207. 3 credits.

COMM300. Communication Ethics

This course investigates the theories and principles of ethical communication applied to a variety of personal and public communications contexts. Students will identify, analyze, and evaluate ethical issues and cases in interpersonal, professional, and mediated contexts. Prerequisite(s): Junior Status, COMM 101 and COMM 200 (with grades of "C-" or better). 3 credits.

CRIM320. Victimology

This course introduces the students to the area of victimology. Students will explore the causes, effects, and societal responses to victimization, the relationship between victims and offenders, as well as the social, psychological, and legal aspects of being a victim while engaging with contemporary theories. Pre-requisite: CRIM 100 with C- or higher. 3 credits.

CTZN320. The Future of the Past

This course examines the making and erasing of public memory in New Orleans through a historical and contemporary cultural lens. Through an immersion learning experience in New Orleans and associated work both pre- and post-travel, students will develop skills for active citizenship, including inquiry from multiple viewpoints, critical evaluation of information, and writing for a general audience. The course will be divided into three components: Pre-Travel, Fieldwork, and Post-Travel. Prerequisite(s): Completion of FHCI. 3 credits. PHCI, WI.

CTZN321. The Future of the Past

This course examines the making and erasing of public memory in New Orleans through the lens of aesthetic expression. Through an immersion learning experience in New Orleans and associated work both pre- and post-travel, students will develop skills for active citizenship, including inquiry from multiple viewpoints, critical evaluation of information, and writing for a general audience. The course will be divided into three components: Pre-Travel, Fieldwork, and Post-Travel. Prerequisite(s): Completion of FAES pillar. PAES, WI. 3 credits.

DATA102. Intro to Data Science

This course introduces the data life-cycle used by data scientists from modeling and collection of empirical evidence to decision-making and answering authentic questions in a variety of domains. Topics will include the elaboration of client problems and queries, assessment of available data sources, analysis protocols for large-scale data sets, and the presentation of findings for non-technical audiences. Basic machine learning approaches will be introduced, along with tools for data mining, visualization, and model evaluation. Prerequisite(s): MATH 171 and CMSC 140, both with a grade of C- or better. 3 credits.

DATA282. Experiential Learning Seminar

This course introduces the steps involved in managing data science projects, client interactions, and effective communication strategies. This course also focuses on analyzing the implementation of the data life-cycle from client-centered consulting projects. Prerequisite: DATA 102. 1 credit.

DATA350. Ethical Issues in Data Science

This course considers the social impact of ethical considerations in data science, examined through the lens of multiple ethical theories. Topics include the ethical examination of the storage and dissemination of data, and the necessary data security issues that may arise; the introduction of biases—intentional or not—related to automated protocols; and the need for transparency. Prerequisite: DATA 102. 3 credits.

DATA481. Prac of Data Sci Consulting I

This is the first course in a two-course sequence in which students will plan and implement an authentic data science project. Students will develop skills critical to successful consultation in relation to the initial stages of project management and planning. The focus is on managing data science projects, optimizing client interactions, and refining the ability to deliver quantitative insights with data-driven decision-making. The course emphasizes working with clients to elaborate their research questions and the availability of data to provide insights into the questions the clients desire to have answered. Prerequisite(s): Restricted to Data Science Majors. DATA 282 with grade of C- or higher and Senior status. 2 credits.

DATA482. Prac of Data Sci Consulting II

This is the second course in a two-course sequence that focuses on managing data science projects, optimizing client interaction, and refining the ability to deliver insights that drive decision making. The course emphasizes collaboration with clients and is designed to allow students to develop skills in leadership, project management, and strategic thinking. Prerequisite: DATA 481. 2 credits.

EASC353. Geography of Virginia

Geographical appraisal of Virginia, including the geology, landforms, soils, climate, economic minerals, original vegetation, and human geography of Virginia, emphasizing settlement of population, agriculture, industries and transportation. 3 lecture periods. 3 credits.

FREN325. Adv Grammar and Composition

Syntax and grammar through written work. Prerequisite(s): FREN 210 or FREN 211 or FREN 212. 3 credits.

FREN326. Adv Conv and Phonetics

Theory and practice in the spoken language. Current techniques and their uses in attaining mastery of oral French. Prerequisite(s): FREN 210 or FREN 211 or FREN 212. 3 credits.

KINS305. Fitness with Community Partner

This course provides hands-on, real-world experience applying kinesiology knowledge in a service-learning context that meets community partner needs. Students’ progress from observation to delivery to co-creation of usable tools that support the health, readiness, and overall well-being of a specific population. Prerequisite(s): KINS 215 & Sophomore Standing. 3 credits.

LSTU390. Directed or Independent Study

MUSC395. Special Topics

Special topics in Music. 1-3 credits.

POSC420. Democracy and Democratization

This class explores the development of democracy, focusing on the theories, definitions, history, and pre-conditions of democratic development. Democratic transitions and consolidations are also considered. 3 credits.

PSYC395. Special Topics Psychology

Selected topics in Psychology. The topics may vary from semester to semester. May be repeated for credit when topics change. 1-3 credits.

SOCL210. Sociology of Indigenous People

This course introduces the sociological study of Indigenous peoples in North America with emphasis on sovereignty, cultural knowledge, and the legacies of colonization. Students will examine the historical and contemporary impacts of genocide, displacement, and systemic inequality, as well as Indigenous strategies of resistance and resilience. Topics include Indigenous ways of knowing, storytelling, and representation in media. Particular attention is given to how sociology contributes to understanding Indigenous experiences and advancing more accurate and equitable portrayals of Native communities. Prerequisite: None. 3 credits.

THEA403. Theatre Practicum

 An observation and participation in the field placement  taking place in a public school setting for a period of 40 hours under the supervision of Longwood. Students will complete reflective journals and other specific reports demonstrating an understanding of the roles of educational professionals and the school culture. Prerequisite(s)/corequisite(s): THEA 401. 1 credit.

PCSD313. Anat Phys Spch Hear Mechanism

Anatomical structures of the human communication system and the physiology of inter-related movements. Prerequisite: PBIO 101 or PBIO 114. 3 credits.
